The spectrometer observed absorption lines common to [hydroxyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxyl "Hydroxyl"), in reflected sunlight, providing evidence of large quantities of water ice, on the lunar surface. The spacecraft showed that concentrations may possibly be as high as 1,000 [ppm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parts_per_million "Parts per million").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pieters2009-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-Pieters2009) Using the mapper's reflectance spectra, indirect lighting of areas in shadow confirmed water ice within 20° latitude of both poles in 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-182) In 2009, _[LCROSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCROSS "LCROSS")_ sent a 2,300kg (5,100lb) impactor into a [permanently shadowed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanently_shadowed_crater "Permanently shadowed crater") polar crater, and detected at least 100kg (220lb) of water in a plume of ejected material.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Planetary-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-Planetary)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Colaprete-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-Colaprete) Another examination of the LCROSS data showed the amount of detected water to be closer to 155±12kg (342±26lb).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Colaprete2010-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-Colaprete2010)

In May 2011, 615–1410 ppm water in [melt inclusions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melt_inclusions "Melt inclusions") in lunar sample 74220 was reported,[&#8202;](#ref-back-hauri-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-hauri) the famous high-titanium "orange glass soil" of volcanic origin collected during the [Apollo 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_17 "Apollo 17") mission in 1972.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hauri-1)[\[179\]](#refnote-hauri) The inclusions were formed during explosive eruptions on the Moon approximately 3.7 billion years ago.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hauri-2)[\[179\]](#refnote-hauri) This concentration is comparable with that of magma in Earth's [upper mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_mantle "Upper mantle").[&#8202;](#ref-back-hauri-3)[\[179\]](#refnote-hauri)

Analysis of the findings of the Moon Mineralogy Mapper (M3) revealed in August 2018 for the first time "definitive evidence" for water-ice on the lunar surface.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Rincon-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-Rincon)[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-188) The data revealed the distinct reflective signatures of water-ice, as opposed to dust and other reflective substances.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Space.com-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-Space.com) The ice deposits were found on the North and South poles, although it is more abundant in the South, where water is trapped in permanently shadowed craters and crevices, allowing it to persist as ice on the surface since they are shielded from the sun.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Rincon-1)[\[180\]](#refnote-Rincon)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Space.com-1)[\[182\]](#refnote-Space.com)

In October 2020, astronomers reported detecting [molecular water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water "Water") on the sunlit surface of the Moon by several independent spacecraft, including the [Stratospheric Observatory for Infrared Astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratospheric_Observatory_for_Infrared_Astronomy "Stratospheric Observatory for Infrared Astronomy") (SOFIA).[&#8202;](#ref-back-NA-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-NA)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NA-20201026poh-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-NA-20201026poh)[&#8202;](#ref-back-WP-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-WP)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-1)[\[186\]](#refnote-NYT)

## Earth–Moon system

See also: [Satellite system (astronomy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_system_(astronomy) "Satellite system (astronomy)") and [Claimed moons of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claimed_moons_of_Earth "Claimed moons of Earth")

### Orbit

Main articles: [Orbit of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit_of_the_Moon "Orbit of the Moon") and [Lunar theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_theory "Lunar theory")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Dscovrepicmoontransitfull.gif/500px-Dscovrepicmoontransitfull.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dscovrepicmoontransitfull.gif) A [Sun-synchronous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun-synchronous_orbit "Sun-synchronous orbit") view of the day-side of Earth from the Sun–Earth [Lagrange point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point "Lagrange point") L1, with Earth rotating and the Moon passing on its orbit in between the observing [DSCOVR satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DSCOVR_satellite "DSCOVR satellite") and Earth, showing [the Moon's far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon "Far side of the Moon") when it is illuminated and the Moon's day-side

The [semi-major axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-major_axis "Semi-major axis") of the geocentric lunar orbit, called the [lunar distance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_distance "Lunar distance"), is approximately 385,000km (239,000mi), or 1.3 [light-seconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-second "Light-second"), comparable to going [around Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumference_of_Earth "Circumference of Earth") approximately 9.6 times.[&#8202;](#ref-back-distance_ratio-0)[\[h\]](#refnote-distance_ratio)[&#8202;](#ref-back-allen-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-allen) The Moon's orbit is not perfectly circular but rather slightly elliptical, with an [orbital eccentricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_eccentricity "Orbital eccentricity") of 0.055. This means the distance between the Moon and Earth varies from around 356,400km (221,500mi) ( [perigee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perigee "Perigee")) to 406,700km (252,700mi) (apogee), making the Moon's distance and apparent size fluctuate up to 14%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-size1-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-size1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-size2-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-size2)

The Earth–Moon system has a shared center of mass, or [barycenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barycenter "Barycenter"), which is located 5,000km (3,100mi) (about 3/4 of Earth's radius) from the Earth's center. Both the Earth and the Moon actually orbit this center of mass, so Earth wobbles around this point for every orbit the Moon makes. This barycenter is slowly moving outward as tidal friction drains energy from the rotating pair, and eventually the barycenter will be outside the Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-els-1)[\[38\]](#refnote-els):69

The Moon makes a complete orbit around Earth with respect to the fixed stars, its [sidereal period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidereal_period "Sidereal period"), about once every 27.3days.[&#8202;](#ref-back-orbpd-0)[\[i\]](#refnote-orbpd) However, because the Earth–Moon system moves at the same time in its orbit around the Sun, it takes slightly longer to return to the same [lunar phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase "Lunar phase"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-199) 29.5days,[&#8202;](#ref-back-synpd-0)[\[j\]](#refnote-synpd)[&#8202;](#ref-back-worldbook-2)[\[79\]](#refnote-worldbook) completing a full cycle as seen from Earth. This [synodic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synodic_period "Synodic period") or synodic month is commonly known as the [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month "Lunar month") and is equal to the length of the [solar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_day "Solar day") on the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Day-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-Day)

The Moon's orbital plane maintains a relatively constant inclination, about 5 degrees, relative to Earth's orbit around the Sun (the [ecliptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic "Ecliptic")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lang2011-2)[\[3\]](#refnote-Lang2011) The direction of this tilt gradually rotates westward, called [nodal precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_precession#Nodal_precession "Lunar precession"), completing a circle once every 18.61years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-tilt-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-tilt) This means that the Moon's orbit changes its tilt relative to Earth's equator from 18 to 28 degrees in that time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-tilt-1)[\[192\]](#refnote-tilt) The shape of the orbit rotates as well, along the plane of the Moon's orbit, called [apsidal precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_precession#Apsidal_precession "Lunar precession").[&#8202;](#ref-back-apsis-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-apsis) Measured from the [intersection points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_node "Lunar node") of the Moon's orbit with the ecliptic plane (which themselves move westward by nodal precession), it rotates eastward with a period of 8.85 years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-apsis-1)[\[193\]](#refnote-apsis) Unlike most [regular satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_satellite "Regular satellite") of other planets, the Moon's orbital plane is closer to the [ecliptic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic_plane "Ecliptic plane") than to their planet's [equatorial plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equatorial_plane "Equatorial plane"). The Moon's orbit is subtly [perturbed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perturbation_(astronomy) "Perturbation (astronomy)") by the Sun and Earth in many small, complex and interacting ways. These follow-on effects are mathematically described by [Cassini's laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassini's_laws "Cassini's laws").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Beletskii2-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-Beletskii2)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Moon_distance_range_to_scale.svg/1920px-Moon_distance_range_to_scale.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Moon_distance_range_to_scale.svg) Minimum, mean and maximum distances of the Moon from Earth with its angular diameter as seen from Earth's surface to scale

### Rotation

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Tidal_locking_of_the_Moon_with_the_Earth.gif/500px-Tidal_locking_of_the_Moon_with_the_Earth.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tidal_locking_of_the_Moon_with_the_Earth.gif) Comparison between the Moon on the left, rotating tidally locked (correct), and with the Moon on the right, without rotation (incorrect)

Due to it being [tidally locked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidally_locked "Tidally locked") to Earth, the Moon has a 1:1 spin–orbit resonance. The rotation–orbit ratio means the Moon's orbital period around Earth is equal to its [rotation period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotation_period "Rotation period"), called synchronous rotation. This results in it always keeping nearly the same face turned towards the Earth as it orbits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-205) The side of the Moon that faces Earth is called the [near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side "Near side"), and the opposite the [far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon "Far side of the Moon"). Since the Moon is not tidally locked to the Sun, lunar daylight and night times both occur around the Moon. The far side is often inaccurately called the "dark side", but it is in fact illuminated as often as the near side: once every 29.5 Earth days. During [dark moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_moon "Dark moon") to [new moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_moon "New moon"), the near side is dark.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-206)

Lunar night is the darkest on the far side and [during lunar eclipses on the near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_eclipses_on_the_Moon "Solar eclipses on the Moon") (and darker than a moonless night on Earth). During its night, the near side is illuminated by [Earthlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthlight "Earthlight") to the extent that lunar surface features may be observable from Earth. Earthshine makes the night on the near side about 43 times brighter, and sometimes even 55 times brighter than a night on Earth illuminated by the light of the full moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-u988-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-u988)

The moon's [axial tilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axial_tilt "Axial tilt") with respect to the [ecliptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic "Ecliptic") is 1.5427°,[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-208) so in each [draconic year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draconic_year "Draconic year") (346.62 days), the sun moves from being 1.5427° north of the lunar equator to being 1.5427° south of it and then back. This is analogous to how, on Earth, the sun's position moves from the [Tropic of Cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Cancer "Tropic of Cancer") to the [Tropic of Capricorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Capricorn "Tropic of Capricorn") and back every [tropical year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_year "Tropical year"). This puts the poles of the moon in darkness (or with only part of the sun visible) for half a draconic year and in sunlight for half a draconic year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-209) The amount of sunlight falling on horizontal areas near the poles depends on the [altitude angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_angle "Altitude angle") of the sun. These seasons have little effect in more equatorial areas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-210)

The Moon originally rotated at a faster rate, but early in its history its rotation slowed and became tidally locked in this orientation as a result of [frictional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friction "Friction") effects associated with [tidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force "Tidal force") deformations caused by Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-211) With time, the energy of rotation of the Moon on its axis was dissipated as heat, until there was no net rotation of the Moon relative to Earth. In 2016, planetary scientists using data collected on the 1998–99 NASA _[Lunar Prospector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Prospector "Lunar Prospector")_ mission found two hydrogen-rich areas (most likely former water ice) on opposite sides of the Moon. It is speculated that these patches were the poles of the Moon billions of years ago before it was tidally locked to Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-212)

### Tidal effects

Further information: [Tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide "Tide"), [Tidal force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force "Tidal force"), and [Theory of tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_tides "Theory of tides")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/77/Tide_animation.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tide_animation.gif) Simplified diagram of Earth bulging, being pulled and stretched toward the Moon by its gravity, which is the main driver of the [tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide "Tide"). The Ocean and Earth are being pulled more where it is closer to the Moon, causing [tidal forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force "Tidal force") to be weaker at the far-side of Earth creating a second bulge and high-tide. The animation shows the change of the Moon's position on its inclined orbit.

The gravitational attraction that Earth and the Moon (as well as the Sun) exert on each other manifests in a slightly greater attraction on the sides closest to each other, resulting in [tidal forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_forces "Tidal forces").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tyson-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-Tyson) [Ocean tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_tides "Ocean tides") are the most widely experienced result of this, but tidal forces also considerably affect other mechanics of Earth, as well as the Moon and their system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-214)[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-215)

The lunar solid crust experiences tides of around 10cm (4in) amplitude over 27days, with three components: a fixed one due to Earth, because they are in [synchronous rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronous_rotation "Synchronous rotation"), a variable tide due to orbital eccentricity and inclination, and a small varying component from the Sun.[&#8202;](#ref-back-touma1994-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-touma1994) The Earth-induced variable component arises from changing distance and [libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration "Libration"), a result of the Moon's orbital eccentricity and inclination (if the Moon's orbit were perfectly circular and un-inclined, there would only be solar tides).[&#8202;](#ref-back-touma1994-1)[\[206\]](#refnote-touma1994) According to recent research, scientists suggest that the Moon's influence on the Earth may contribute to maintaining [Earth's magnetic field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_magnetic_field "Earth's magnetic field").[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-217)

The cumulative effects of stress built up by these tidal forces produces [moonquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonquakes "Moonquakes"). Moonquakes are much less common and weaker than are earthquakes, although moonquakes can last for up to an hour– significantly longer than terrestrial quakes– because of scattering of the seismic vibrations in the dry fragmented upper crust.[&#8202;](#ref-back-quake-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-quake) The existence of moonquakes was an unexpected discovery from [seismometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seismometer "Seismometer") placed on the Moon by [Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program "Apollo program") [astronauts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronaut "Astronaut") from 1969 through 1972.[&#8202;](#ref-back-quake-1)[\[208\]](#refnote-quake)

The most commonly known effect of tidal forces is elevated sea levels called ocean tides.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lambeck1977-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-Lambeck1977) While the Moon exerts most of the tidal forces, the Sun also exerts tidal forces and therefore contributes to the tides as much as 40% of the Moon's tidal force; producing in interplay the [spring and neap tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_tide "Spring tide").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lambeck1977-1)[\[209\]](#refnote-Lambeck1977)

The tides are two bulges in the Earth's oceans, one on the side facing the Moon and the other on the side opposite. As the Earth rotates on its axis, one of the ocean bulges (high tide) is held in place "under" the Moon, while another such tide is opposite. The tide under the Moon is explained by the Moon's gravity being stronger on the water close to it. The tide on the opposite side can be explained either by the centrifugal force as the Earth orbits the [barycenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barycenter "Barycenter") or by the water's inertia as the Moon's gravity is stronger on the solid Earth close to it and it is pulled away from the farther water.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-220)[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-221)

Thus, there are two high tides, and two low tides in about 24 hours.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lambeck1977-2)[\[209\]](#refnote-Lambeck1977) Since the Moon is orbiting the Earth in the same direction of the Earth's rotation, the high tides occur about every 12 hours and 25 minutes; the 25 minutes is due to the Moon's time to orbit the Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-222)

If the Earth were a water world (one with no continents) it would produce a tide of only one meter, and that tide would be very predictable, but the ocean tides are greatly modified by other effects:

- the frictional coupling of water to Earth's rotation through the ocean floors
- the [inertia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertia "Inertia") of water's movement
- ocean basins that grow shallower near land
- the sloshing of water between different ocean basins[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-223)

### System evolution

Further information: [Tidal acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_acceleration "Tidal acceleration")

Delays in the tidal peaks of both ocean and solid-body tides cause [torque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torque "Torque") in opposition to the Earth's rotation. This "drains" [angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_momentum "Angular momentum") and rotational [kinetic energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinetic_energy "Kinetic energy") from Earth's rotation, slowing the Earth's rotation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lambeck1977-3)[\[209\]](#refnote-Lambeck1977)[&#8202;](#ref-back-touma1994-2)[\[206\]](#refnote-touma1994) 3.2 billion years ago, during the [Archean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archean "Archean"), the solar day was only around 13 hours. The angular momentum, lost from the rotation of the Earth, is transferred to the Moon in a process known as [tidal acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_acceleration "Tidal acceleration"), which lifts the Moon into a higher orbit while lowering orbital speed around the Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-m870-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-m870)

Thus the distance between Earth and Moon is increasing, and the Earth's rotation is slowing in reaction.[&#8202;](#ref-back-touma1994-3)[\[206\]](#refnote-touma1994) Measurements from laser reflectors left during the Apollo missions ( [lunar ranging experiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Laser_Ranging_experiments "Lunar Laser Ranging experiments")) have found that the Moon's distance increases by 38mm (1.5in) per year (roughly the rate at which human fingernails grow).[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-226)[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-227) [Atomic clocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_clock "Atomic clock") show that Earth's Day lengthens by about 17 [microseconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsecond "Microsecond") every year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-228)[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-229)[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-230)

Over time this tidal drag causes the Earth's rotation and the Moon's orbital period to match, resulting in the lighter body becoming tidally locked (as the Moon currently is). Theoretically, in 50 billion years,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Universe_Today_2016-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-Universe_Today_2016) the Earth's rotation will slow to match the Moon's orbital period, causing the Earth to always present the same side to the Moon. Long before this would happen, however, the Sun will become a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant#The_Sun_as_a_red_giant "Red giant"), possibly engulfing the Earth and the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-232)[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-233) If the Earth–Moon system survives this engulfment, as a 2026 study suggests,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Esseldeurs_et_al_2026-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-Esseldeurs_et_al_2026) the drag from the solar atmosphere could cause the orbit of the Moon to decay. Once the orbit of the Moon closes to a distance of 18,470km (11,480mi), it will cross Earth's [Roche limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_limit "Roche limit") and the tidal interaction with Earth would break the Moon apart. It would turn into a [ring system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_system "Ring system"), with most of the orbiting rings decaying and falling to Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-f170-1)[\[77\]](#refnote-f170)

## Observation

Main article: [Lunar observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_observation "Lunar observation")

### Apparent size and position

The Moon appears in Earth's sky differently depending on the position of observation on Earth and the time of [lunar year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_year "Lunar year"), [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month "Lunar month") and day on Earth. During a year the Moon [culminates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culmination "Culmination") during a day at different [altitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_(astronomy) "Altitude (astronomy)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-235) The full Moon appears highest in the sky during winter and lowest during summer, unlike the Sun,[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-236) which is true for the seasons of both of Earth's northern and southern hemispheres.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

At the [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Pole "North Pole") and [South Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pole "South Pole") the Moon is 24 hours above the horizon for two weeks every [tropical month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_month "Tropical month") (about 27.3 days), comparable to the [polar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_day "Polar day") of the [tropical year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_year "Tropical year"). [Zooplankton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zooplankton "Zooplankton") in the [Arctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic "Arctic") use [moonlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlight "Moonlight") when the Sun is [below the horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_night "Polar night") for months on end.[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-237)

On average the Moon's [angular diameter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_diameter "Angular diameter") is about 0.52°, roughly the same apparent size as the Sun (see [§Eclipses](#Eclipses)). Its apparent size and brightness vary due to its elliptical orbit, appearing up to 30% brighter and 14% larger at perigee compared to apogee. In addition, a purely psychological effect, known as the [Moon illusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_illusion "Moon illusion"), makes the Moon appear larger when close to the [horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizon "Horizon").[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-238)

### Orientation and appearance

[![Over one lunar month more than half of the Moon's surface can be seen from Earth's surface.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Lunar_libration_with_phase_Oct_2007_HD.gif/500px-Lunar_libration_with_phase_Oct_2007_HD.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lunar_libration_with_phase_Oct_2007_HD.gif) [Libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration "Libration"), the slight variation in the Moon's [apparent size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_size "Apparent size") and viewing angle over a single lunar month as viewed from somewhere on the Earth's Northern Hemisphere.

The apparent orientation of the Moon depends on its position in the sky and the hemisphere of the Earth from which it is being viewed. In the [Northern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere "Northern Hemisphere") it appears upside down compared to the view from the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere "Southern Hemisphere").[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-239) Sometimes the "horns" of a crescent moon appear to be pointing more upwards than sideways. This phenomenon is called a [wet moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_moon "Wet moon") and occurs more frequently in the [tropics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropics "Tropics").[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-240)

The tidally locked [synchronous rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronous_rotation "Synchronous rotation") of the Moon as it orbits the Earth results in only one side of the Moon, its so-called [near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side "Near side"), being visible from Earth. That said, while the movement of the Moon is in resonance, it still is not without nuances such as [libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration "Libration"), resulting in slightly changing perspectives, making over time and location on Earth about 59% of the Moon's surface visible from Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Libration-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-Libration) Due to lunar libration, it is sometimes possible to see slightly more than the full moon (up to about 101%) or a small part of its far side (up to 5%).[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-242)

#### Observational phenomena

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Lunar_impact_flash.jpg/500px-Lunar_impact_flash.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lunar_impact_flash.jpg) [Lunar impact flash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transient_lunar_phenomenon#Impact_flash "Transient lunar phenomenon") image shown superimposed on the Moon

There has been historical controversy over whether observed features on the Moon's surface change over time. Today, many of these claims are thought to be illusory, resulting from observation under different lighting conditions, poor [astronomical seeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_seeing "Astronomical seeing"), or inadequate drawings. However, [outgassing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outgassing "Outgassing") does occasionally occur and could be responsible for a minor percentage of the reported [lunar transient phenomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_transient_phenomena "Lunar transient phenomena"). Recently, it has been suggested that a roughly 3km (1.9mi) diameter region of the lunar surface was modified by a gas release event about a million years ago.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-243)[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-244)

### Phases

Main article: [Lunar phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase "Lunar phase")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Moon_phases_en.jpg/1920px-Moon_phases_en.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Moon_phases_en.jpg) The monthly changes in the angle between the direction of sunlight and view from Earth, and the [phases of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase "Lunar phase") that result, as viewed from the [Northern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere "Northern Hemisphere"). The [Earth–Moon distance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth%E2%80%93Moon_distance "Earth–Moon distance") is not to scale.

The Moon rotates, as it orbits Earth, changing orientation toward the Sun, experiencing a [lunar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_day "Lunar day"). A lunar day is equal to one [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month "Lunar month") (one synodic orbit around Earth). The changing position of the illumination of the Moon by the Sun during a lunar day is observable from Earth as the changing [lunar phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase "Lunar phase"). The phases of the moon follow a repeating cycle, with its starting point at new moon. In the first half of the cycle, the sequence is new moon, crescent, quarter, gibbous, and full moon. In the second half of the cycle, the sequence is reversed, then the cycle repeats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-r040-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-r040)

The brightness and apparent size of the Moon changes also due to its elliptic orbit around Earth. Consequently, given the same phase, the Moon's brightness also varies by up to 30% between apogee and perigee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-246) At [perigee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perigee "Perigee") (closest), since the Moon is up to 14% closer to Earth than at [apogee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apogee "Apogee") (most distant), it subtends a [solid angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_angle "Solid angle") which is up to 30% larger. A full (or new) moon at such a position is called a [supermoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermoon "Supermoon").[&#8202;](#ref-back-size1-1)[\[188\]](#refnote-size1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-size2-1)[\[189\]](#refnote-size2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-247)

Each of the four intermediate phases of the Moon lasts, on average, about seven days (approximately 7.38 days), but this duration can vary by about ±11% due to the change in the distance between the Moon and Earth between apogee (farthest point) and perigee (closest point).\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The number of days since the most recent new moon is called the Moon's age.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-248) A complete cycle of lunar phases is known as a lunation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-249)

The approximate age and phase of the Moon for any date can be calculated by determining the number of days since a known new moon (e.g., January 1, 1900, or August 11, 1999) and dividing that value by the average length of a synodic month (29.53059 days).[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-250) The remainder of the division represents the Moon's age. This method assumes a perfectly circular orbit and does not consider the exact time of the new moon, so results may be off by a few hours. Accuracy decreases as the date moves away from the reference date. This simplified calculation is suitable for many purposes, but more precise applications require more complex calculation.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### True albedo and color of the surface

See also: [Moonlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlight "Moonlight") and [Halo (optical phenomenon)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halo_(optical_phenomenon) "Halo (optical phenomenon)")

The Moon's appearance is shaped by its reflective properties and surface composition. Its [albedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albedo "Albedo") is about 0.12, meaning it reflects 12% of the light that hits it. This is similar to an asteroid's albedo.[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-251) [Chromophore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromophore "Chromophore") elements, iron and titanium, on the Moon surface absorb light, making areas like the lunar mares darker (lower albedo) than the highlands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-252)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Two_Lunar_Phases.jpg/500px-Two_Lunar_Phases.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Two_Lunar_Phases.jpg) The changing apparent color of the Moon, filtered by Earth's atmosphere

The Moon's true color, without atmospheric effects, is a muted brownish-gray.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-253) From Earth, the Moon's color can be altered by the atmosphere, at times appearing red or blue. It may appear red during a [lunar eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_eclipse "Lunar eclipse"), because of the red spectrum of the Sun's light being [refracted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refracted "Refracted") onto the Moon by Earth's atmosphere. Because of this red color, lunar eclipses are also sometimes called blood moons. The Moon can also seem red when it appears at low angles and through a thick atmosphere. The Moon may also appear blue depending on the presence of certain particles in the air,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Science_Mission_Directorate_2020-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-Science_Mission_Directorate_2020) such as volcanic particles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-255) Because the words "blood moon" and " [blue moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_moon "Blue moon")" can also be used to refer to specific [full moons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_moon "Full moon") of the year, they do not always refer to the presence of red or blue [moonlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlight "Moonlight").[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-256)[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-257)

### Eclipses

Main articles: [Solar eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_eclipse "Solar eclipse"), [Lunar eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_eclipse "Lunar eclipse"), and [Eclipse cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclipse_cycle "Eclipse cycle")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Solar_eclipse_1999_4.jpg/330px-Solar_eclipse_1999_4.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Solar_eclipse_1999_4_NR.jpg)

A [solar eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_eclipse "Solar eclipse") causes the Sun to be covered, revealing the white [corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_corona "Stellar corona").

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Full_Eclipse_of_the_Moon_as_seen_in_from_Irvine%2C_CA%2C_USA_%2852075715442%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Full_Eclipse_of_the_Moon_as_seen_in_from_Irvine%2C_CA%2C_USA_%2852075715442%29_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Full_Eclipse_of_the_Moon_as_seen_in_from_Irvine,_CA,_USA_(52075715442)_(cropped).jpg)

The Moon, tinted reddish, during a [lunar eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_eclipse "Lunar eclipse")

Eclipses only occur when the Sun, Earth, and Moon are all in a straight line (termed " [syzygy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syzygy_(astronomy) "Syzygy (astronomy)")"). [Solar eclipses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_eclipse "Solar eclipse") occur at [new moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_moon "New moon"), when the Moon is between the Sun and Earth. In contrast, [lunar eclipses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_eclipse "Lunar eclipse") occur at full moon, when Earth is between the Sun and Moon. The apparent size of the Moon is roughly the same as that of the Sun, with both being viewed at close to one-half a degree wide. The Sun is much larger than the Moon, but it is the vastly greater distance that gives it the same apparent size as the much closer and much smaller Moon from the perspective of Earth. The variations in apparent size, due to the non-circular orbits, are nearly the same as well, though occurring in different cycles. This makes possible both [total](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_eclipse "Total eclipse") (with the Moon appearing larger than the Sun) and [annular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annular_eclipse "Annular eclipse") (with the Moon appearing smaller than the Sun) solar eclipses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-258) In a total eclipse, the Moon completely covers the disc of the Sun and the [solar corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_corona "Solar corona") becomes visible to the [naked eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naked_eye "Naked eye").[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-259)

Because the distance between the Moon and Earth is very slowly increasing over time,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lambeck1977-4)[\[209\]](#refnote-Lambeck1977) the angular diameter of the Moon is decreasing. As the Sun evolves toward becoming a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant "Red giant"), its size and its apparent diameter in the sky are slowly increasing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-size_changes-0)[\[k\]](#refnote-size_changes) The combination of these two changes means that hundreds of millions of years ago, the Moon would always completely cover the Sun on solar eclipses, and no annular eclipses were possible. Likewise, hundreds of millions of years in the future, the Moon will no longer cover the Sun completely, and total solar eclipses will not occur.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fourmilab-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-fourmilab)

As the Moon's orbit around Earth is inclined by about 5.145° (5°9′) to the [orbit of Earth around the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic "Ecliptic"), eclipses do not occur at every full and new moon. For an eclipse to occur, the Moon must be near the intersection of the two orbital planes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-eclipse-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-eclipse) The periodicity and recurrence of eclipses of the Sun by the Moon, and of the Moon by Earth, is described by the [saros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saros_(astronomy) "Saros (astronomy)"), which has a period of approximately 18years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-263)

Because the Moon continuously blocks the view of a half-degree-wide circular area of the sky,[&#8202;](#ref-back-area-0)[\[l\]](#refnote-area)[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-265) the related phenomenon of [occultation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occultation "Occultation") occurs when a bright star or planet passes behind the Moon and is occulted: hidden from view. In this way, a solar eclipse is an occultation of the Sun. Because the Moon is comparatively close to Earth, occultations of individual stars are not visible everywhere on the planet, nor at the same time. Because of the [precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precession "Precession") of the lunar orbit, each year different stars are occulted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-266)

## Exploration of the Moon

Main article: [Exploration of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Moon "Exploration of the Moon")

See also: [List of spacecraft that orbited the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_spacecraft_that_orbited_the_Moon "List of spacecraft that orbited the Moon"), [List of missions to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_missions_to_the_Moon "List of missions to the Moon"), and [List of lunar probes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lunar_probes "List of lunar probes")

### Pre-telescopic observation (before 1609)

It is possible that the waxing and waning of the [lunar phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phases "Lunar phases") were tracked to keep time in the oldest cave paintings of bulls and geometric shapes, from up to 40,000 years ago,[&#8202;](#ref-back-e093-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-e093) or by the use of [tally sticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tally_stick "Tally stick") from 20–30,000 years ago.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Burton2011-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-Burton2011)
Aspects of the Moon were identified and aggregated in [lunar deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_deities "Lunar deities") from prehistoric times and were eventually documented and put into symbols from the very first instances of writing in the 4th millennium BC. One of the earliest-discovered possible depictions of the Moon is a 3,000 BCE rock carving _Orthostat 47_ at [Knowth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowth "Knowth"), Ireland.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Knowth-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-Knowth)[&#8202;](#ref-back-spacetoday-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-spacetoday) The crescent depicting the Moon as with the lunar deity [Nanna/Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_(mythology) "Sin (mythology)") have been found from the 3rd millennium BC.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BlackGreen1992-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-BlackGreen1992)

The oldest named astronomer and poet [Enheduanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enheduanna "Enheduanna"), [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire "Akkadian Empire") high priestess to the lunar deity Nanna/Sin and princess, daughter of [Sargon the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_the_Great "Sargon the Great") (c.2334 – c.2279 BCE), had the Moon tracked in her chambers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-c099-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-c099) The oldest identified depiction of the Moon in an astronomical relation to other astronomical features is the [Nebra sky disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebra_sky_disc "Nebra sky disc") from c.1800–1600 BCE, depicting features like the [Pleiades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleiades "Pleiades") next to the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-g361-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-g361)[&#8202;](#ref-back-k874-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-k874)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Nebra_solstice_2.jpg/500px-Nebra_solstice_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nebra_solstice_2.jpg) The Nebra sky disc (c.1800–1600 BCE), found near a possible [astronomical complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_complex "Astronomical complex"), most likely depicting the Sun or full Moon, the Moon as a crescent, the Pleiades and the summer and winter solstices as strips of gold on the side of the disc,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Meller_2021-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-Meller_2021)[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-276) with the top representing the horizon[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-277) and [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North "North").

The [ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece "Ancient Greece") philosopher [Anaxagoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaxagoras "Anaxagoras") (d.428 BC) reasoned that the Sun and Moon were both giant spherical rocks, and that the Moon reflected the Sun's light.[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-278)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Needham1986-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-Needham1986):227 Elsewhere in the 5th century BC to 4th century BC, [Babylonian astronomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_astronomers "Babylonian astronomers") had recorded the 18-year [Saros cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saros_cycle "Saros cycle") of [lunar eclipses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_eclipse "Lunar eclipse"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-280) and [Indian astronomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_astronomy "Indian astronomy") had described the Moon's monthly elongation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sarma-Ast-Ind-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-Sarma-Ast-Ind) The [Chinese astronomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_astronomer "Chinese astronomer") [Shi Shen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi_Shen "Shi Shen")( [fl.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fl. "Fl.") 4th century BC) gave instructions for predicting solar and lunar eclipses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Needham1986-1)[\[267\]](#refnote-Needham1986):411

In [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle "Aristotle")'s (384–322BC) [description of the universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Heavens "On the Heavens"), the Moon marked the boundary between the spheres of the mutable elements (earth, water, air and fire), and the imperishable stars of [aether](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aether_(classical_element) "Aether (classical element)"), an influential concept from [Aristotelian physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_physics "Aristotelian physics") that would dominate for centuries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-282) [Archimedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archimedes "Archimedes") (287–212 BC) designed a planetarium that could calculate the motions of the Moon and other objects in the Solar System.[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-283) In the 2nd century BC, [Seleucus of Seleucia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucus_of_Seleucia "Seleucus of Seleucia") correctly thought that tides were due to the attraction of the Moon, and that their height depends on the Moon's position relative to the Sun.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-284) In the same century, [Aristarchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Sizes_and_Distances_(Aristarchus) "On the Sizes and Distances (Aristarchus)") computed the size and distance of the Moon from Earth, obtaining a value of about twenty times the radius of Earth for the distance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-285)

The Chinese of the [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_dynasty "Han dynasty") believed the Moon to be energy equated to _[qi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi "Qi")_ and their 'radiating influence' theory recognized that the light of the Moon was merely a reflection of the Sun; [Jing Fang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jing_Fang "Jing Fang") (78–37BC) noted the sphericity of the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Needham1986-2)[\[267\]](#refnote-Needham1986):413–414[Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy "Ptolemy") (90–168AD) greatly improved on the numbers of Aristarchus, calculating a mean distance of 59times Earth's radius and a diameter of 0.292Earth diameters, close to the correct values of about 60 and 0.273 respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-286) In 510AD, the Indian astronomer [Aryabhata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryabhata "Aryabhata") mentioned in his _[Aryabhatiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryabhatiya "Aryabhatiya")_ that reflected sunlight is the cause of the shining of the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hayashi08Aryabhata-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-Hayashi08Aryabhata)[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-288) The astronomer and physicist [Ibn al-Haytham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Haytham "Ibn al-Haytham") (965–1039) found that sunlight was not reflected from the Moon like a mirror, but that light was emitted from every part of the Moon's sunlit surface in all directions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-289) [Shen Kuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shen_Kuo "Shen Kuo") (1031–1095) of the [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty "Song dynasty") created an allegory equating the [waxing and waning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waxing_and_waning "Waxing and waning") of the Moon to a round ball of reflective silver that, when doused with white powder and viewed from the side, would appear to be a crescent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Needham1986-3)[\[267\]](#refnote-Needham1986):415–416 During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages "Middle Ages"), before the invention of the telescope, the Moon was increasingly recognized as a sphere, though many believed that it was "perfectly smooth".[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-290)

### Telescopic exploration (1609–1959)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon_%28cropped%29.png/500px-Galileo%27s_sketches_of_the_moon_%28cropped%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Galileo's_sketches_of_the_moon_(cropped).png) The first published sketches of one of the first telescopic observations of the Moon, from the paper _[Sidereus Nuncius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidereus_Nuncius "Sidereus Nuncius")_ (1610) by [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei "Galileo Galilei"), providing among other findings the first descriptions of the Moon's topography

The [telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telescope "Telescope") was developed and reported on in 1608.[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-291) The first record of telescopic astronomy and rough mapping of the Moon's features is from early summer 1609 by [Thomas Harriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Harriot "Thomas Harriot"), but which he did not publish.[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-292) At the same time [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei "Galileo Galilei") too started to use telescopes to observe the sky and the Moon, recording later that year more detailed observations and crucial conclusions, such as that the Moon was not smooth, featuring mountains and craters, which he published in 1610 in his ground-breaking and soon widely discussed book _[Sidereus Nuncius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidereus_Nuncius "Sidereus Nuncius")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-293)

Later in the 17th century, the efforts of [Giovanni Battista Riccioli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Riccioli "Giovanni Battista Riccioli") and [Francesco Maria Grimaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesco_Maria_Grimaldi "Francesco Maria Grimaldi") led to the system of naming of lunar features in use today.[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-294) The more exact 1834–1836 _[Mappa Selenographica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mappa_Selenographica "Mappa Selenographica")_ of [Wilhelm Beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Beer "Wilhelm Beer") and [Johann Heinrich von Mädler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Heinrich_von_M%C3%A4dler "Johann Heinrich von Mädler"), and their associated 1837 book _Der Mond_, the first [trigonometrically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry "Trigonometry") accurate study of lunar features, included the heights of more than a thousand mountains, and introduced the study of the Moon at accuracies possible in earthly geography.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-295) Lunar craters, first noted by Galileo, were thought to be [volcanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic "Volcanic") until the 1870s proposal of [Richard Proctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Proctor "Richard Proctor") that they were formed by collisions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-worldbook-3)[\[79\]](#refnote-worldbook) This view gained support in 1892 from the experimentation of geologist [Grove Karl Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grove_Karl_Gilbert "Grove Karl Gilbert"), and from comparative studies from 1920 to the 1940s,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hall1977-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-Hall1977) leading to the development of [lunar stratigraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_geologic_timescale "Lunar geologic timescale"), which by the 1950s was becoming a new and growing branch of [astrogeology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrogeology "Astrogeology").[&#8202;](#ref-back-worldbook-4)[\[79\]](#refnote-worldbook)

### First missions to the Moon (1959–1976)

See also: [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race "Space Race") and [Moon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing "Moon landing")

After [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") the first [launch systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Launch_system "Launch system") were developed and by the end of the 1950s they reached capabilities that allowed the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") and the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") to launch [spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft "Spacecraft") into space.[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-297) The [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") fueled a closely followed development of launch systems by the two states, resulting in the so-called [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race "Space Race") and its later phase the Moon Race, accelerating efforts and interest in [exploration of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Moon "Exploration of the Moon").[&#8202;](#ref-back-rmg-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-rmg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Luna_3_moon.jpg/500px-Luna_3_moon.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Luna_3_moon.jpg) First view of the [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon "Far side of the Moon"), taken by [Luna 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_3 "Luna 3"), October 7, 1959. Clearly visible is [Mare Moscoviense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Moscoviense "Mare Moscoviense") (top right) and a mare triplet of [Mare Crisium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Crisium "Mare Crisium"), [Mare Marginis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Marginis "Mare Marginis") and [Mare Smythii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Smythii "Mare Smythii") (left center).

After the first spaceflight of [Sputnik 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_1 "Sputnik 1") in 1957 during [International Geophysical Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Geophysical_Year "International Geophysical Year") the spacecraft of the Soviet Union's [_Luna_ program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_programme "Luna programme") were the first to accomplish a number of goals. Following three unnamed failed missions in 1958,[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-299) the first human-made object _[Luna 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_1 "Luna 1")_ escaped Earth's gravity and passed the Moon on 4 January 1959.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NSSDCTrajectory-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-NSSDCTrajectory) Later that year the first human-made object _[Luna 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_2 "Luna 2")_ reached the Moon's surface by [intentionally impacting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lander_(spacecraft)#Impactors "Lander (spacecraft)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-301) By the end of the year _[Luna 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_3 "Luna 3")_ reached as the first human-made object the normally occluded [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon "Far side of the Moon"), taking the first photographs of it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-302) The first spacecraft to perform a successful lunar [soft landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lander_(spacecraft) "Lander (spacecraft)") was _[Luna 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_9 "Luna 9")_[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-303) and the first vehicle to orbit the Moon was _[Luna 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_10 "Luna 10")_, both in 1966.[&#8202;](#ref-back-worldbook-5)[\[79\]](#refnote-worldbook)

[![The small blue-white semicircle of Earth, almost glowing with color in the blackness of space, rising over the limb of the desolate, cratered surface of the Moon.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/NASA-Apollo8-Dec24-Earthrise.jpg/500px-NASA-Apollo8-Dec24-Earthrise.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NASA-Apollo8-Dec24-Earthrise.jpg) _[Earthrise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthrise "Earthrise")_, by NASA astronaut [William Anders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Anders "William Anders"), the first color [image of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_first_images_of_Earth_from_space "Timeline of first images of Earth from space") taken by a human from the Moon, during [Apollo 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_8 "Apollo 8") (1968), the first time a crewed spacecraft left Earth orbit and reached another [astronomical body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_body "Astronomical body")

Following President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy "John F. Kennedy")'s 1961 commitment to a crewed Moon landing before the end of the decade, the United States, under NASA leadership, launched a series of uncrewed probes to develop an understanding of the lunar surface in preparation for human missions: the [Jet Propulsion Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Propulsion_Laboratory "Jet Propulsion Laboratory")'s [Ranger program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranger_program "Ranger program"), the [Lunar Orbiter program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Orbiter_program "Lunar Orbiter program") and the [Surveyor program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_program "Surveyor program").[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-304) The crewed [Apollo program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program "Apollo program") was developed in parallel; after a series of uncrewed and crewed tests of the Apollo spacecraft in Earth orbit, and spurred on by a potential [Soviet lunar human landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_human_lunar_programs "Soviet human lunar programs"), in 1968 [Apollo 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_8 "Apollo 8") made the first human mission to lunar orbit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-305)The first Earthlings, two tortoises, had circled the Moon three months earlier on the Soviet Union's [Zond 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zond_5 "Zond 5"), followed by turtles on [Zond 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zond_6 "Zond 6").[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-306)

The first time a person landed on the Moon and any extraterrestrial body was when [Neil Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Armstrong "Neil Armstrong"), the commander of the American mission [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11 "Apollo 11"), set foot on the Moon at 02:56UTC on July 21, 1969.[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-307) Considered the culmination of the [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race "Space Race"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-CNN) an estimated 500million people worldwide watched the transmission by the [Apollo TV camera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_TV_camera "Apollo TV camera"), the largest television audience for a live broadcast at that time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-309)[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-310) While at the same time another mission, the robotic sample return mission [Luna 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_15 "Luna 15") by the Soviet Union had been in orbit around the Moon, becoming together with Apollo 11 the first ever case of two extraterrestrial missions being conducted at the same time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-311)

The Apollo missions 11 to 17 (except [Apollo 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_13 "Apollo 13"), which aborted its planned lunar landing) removed 380.05 kilograms (837.87lb) of lunar rock and soil in 2,196 [separate samples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_rock "Moon rock").[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-312)
Scientific instrument packages were installed on the lunar surface during all the Apollo landings. Long-lived [instrument stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Lunar_Surface_Experiments_Package "Apollo Lunar Surface Experiments Package"), including heat flow probes, [seismometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seismometer "Seismometer"), and [magnetometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetometer "Magnetometer"), were installed at the [Apollo 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_12 "Apollo 12"), [14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_14 "Apollo 14"), [15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_15 "Apollo 15"), [16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_16 "Apollo 16"), and [17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_17 "Apollo 17") landing sites. Direct transmission of data to Earth concluded in late 1977 because of budgetary considerations,[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-313)[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-314) but as the stations' [lunar laser ranging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_laser_ranging "Lunar laser ranging") corner-cube retroreflector arrays are passive instruments, they are still being used.[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-315)
Apollo 17 in 1972 was the last crewed mission to the Moon's orbit or surface, and the last to the Moon's vicinity until the [Artemis II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_II "Artemis II") lunar flyby in 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-316) [Explorer 49](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explorer_49 "Explorer 49") in 1973 was the last dedicated U.S. lunar probe until 1994.[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-317)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/AS12-48-7133_%2821470506269%29.jpg/500px-AS12-48-7133_%2821470506269%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AS12-48-7133_(21470506269).jpg) An example of the robotic and human exploration of the Moon in the first [visit of a separate mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_rendezvous "Space rendezvous") beyond [Low Earth Orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_Orbit "Low Earth Orbit") (1969),[&#8202;](#ref-back-g936-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-g936) [Apollo 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_12 "Apollo 12") astronaut [Pete Conrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Conrad "Pete Conrad") checking on [Surveyor 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_3 "Surveyor 3") at their shared [lunar base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_base "Lunar base"), with the Apollo 12 _Intrepid_ lander and S-band antenna in the distant background

The Soviet Union continued sending robotic missions to the Moon until 1976, deploying in 1970 with [Luna 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_17 "Luna 17") the first remote controlled [rover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rover_(space_exploration) "Rover (space exploration)") [Lunokhod 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunokhod_1 "Lunokhod 1") on an extraterrestrial surface, and collecting and returning 0.3kg of rock and soil samples with three _Luna_ [sample return missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sample_return_mission "Sample return mission") ( _[Luna 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_16 "Luna 16")_ in 1970, _[Luna 20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_20 "Luna 20")_ in 1972, and _[Luna 24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_24 "Luna 24")_ in 1976).[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-319)

### Moon Treaty and explorational absence (1976–1990)

Main article: [Moon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Treaty "Moon Treaty")

Following the [last Soviet mission to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_24 "Luna 24") of 1976, there was little further lunar exploration for fourteen years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-timeline-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-timeline) Astronautics had shifted its focus towards the exploration of the [inner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Solar_System "Inner Solar System") (e.g. [Venera program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venera_program "Venera program")) and [outer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Solar_System "Outer Solar System") (e.g. [Pioneer 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pioneer_10 "Pioneer 10"), 1972) [Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_System "Solar System") [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet "Planet"), but also towards [Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geocentric_orbit "Geocentric orbit"), developing and continuously operating, beside [communication satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communication_satellite "Communication satellite"), [Earth observation satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_observation_satellite "Earth observation satellite") (e.g. [Landsat program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landsat_program "Landsat program"), 1972), [space telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_telescope "Space telescope") and particularly [space stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_station "Space station") (e.g. [Salyut program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salyut_program "Salyut program"), 1971).[&#8202;](#ref-back-321-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-321)

Negotiation in 1979 of [Moon treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_treaty "Moon treaty"), and its subsequent ratification in 1984 was the only major activity regarding the Moon until 1990.[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-322)[&#8202;](#ref-back-timeline-1)[\[308\]](#refnote-timeline)

### Renewed exploration (1990–present)

In 1990 _[Hiten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiten_(spacecraft) "Hiten (spacecraft)")_ – _Hagoromo_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-323) the first dedicated lunar mission since 1976, reached the Moon's orbit. Sent by Japan, it became the first lunar mission that was not a Soviet Union or U.S. mission.[&#8202;](#ref-back-timeline-2)[\[308\]](#refnote-timeline)

In 1994, the U.S. dedicated a mission to fly a spacecraft ( _[Clementine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clementine_(spacecraft) "Clementine (spacecraft)")_) to the Moon again for the first time since 1973. This mission obtained the first near-global topographic map of the Moon, and the first global [multispectral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multispectral "Multispectral") images of the lunar surface.[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-324) In 1998, this was followed by the _[Lunar Prospector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Prospector "Lunar Prospector")_ mission, whose instruments indicated the presence of excess hydrogen at the lunar poles, which is likely to have been caused by the presence of water ice in the upper few meters of the regolith within permanently shadowed craters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-325)

The next years saw a row of first missions to the Moon by a new group of states actively exploring the Moon.
Between 2004 and 2006 the first spacecraft by the [European Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Space_Agency "European Space Agency") (ESA) ( [SMART-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SMART-1 "SMART-1")) orbited the Moon, recording the first detailed survey of chemical elements on the lunar surface.[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-326)
The [Chinese Lunar Exploration Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Lunar_Exploration_Program "Chinese Lunar Exploration Program") reached the Moon for the first time with the orbiter _[Chang'e 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_1 "Chang'e 1")_ (2007–2009),[&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-327) obtaining a full image map of the Moon.
[India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Space_Research_Organisation "Indian Space Research Organisation") reached, orbited, and impacted the Moon in 2008 for the first time with its [Chandrayaan-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-1 "Chandrayaan-1") and [Moon Impact Probe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Impact_Probe "Moon Impact Probe"), becoming the fifth and sixth state to do so, creating a high-resolution chemical, mineralogical and photo-geological map of the lunar surface, and confirming the presence of [water molecules in lunar soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_water "Lunar water").[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-328)

The U.S. launched the [Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter (LRO)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Reconnaissance_Orbiter "Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter") and the [LCROSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCROSS "LCROSS") impactor on June 18, 2009. LCROSS completed its mission by making a planned and widely observed impact in the crater [Cabeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabeus "Cabeus") on October 9, 2009,[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-329) whereas _LRO_ is currently in operation, obtaining precise lunar [altimetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altimetry "Altimetry") and high-resolution imagery.[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-330)

China continued its lunar program in 2010 with [Chang'e 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_2 "Chang'e 2"), mapping the surface at a higher resolution over an eight-month period, and in 2013 with [Chang'e 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_3 "Chang'e 3"), a lunar [lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lander_(spacecraft) "Lander (spacecraft)") along with a [lunar rover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_rover "Lunar rover") named _[Yutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yutu_(rover) "Yutu (rover)")_ ( [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language"):玉兔; lit.'Jade Rabbit'). This was the first lunar rover mission since _[Lunokhod 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunokhod_2 "Lunokhod 2")_ in 1973 and the first lunar [soft landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing "Soft landing") since _[Luna 24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_24 "Luna 24")_ in 1976, making China the third country to achieve this.[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-331)

In 2014 the first privately funded probe, the [Manfred Memorial Moon Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manfred_Memorial_Moon_Mission "Manfred Memorial Moon Mission"), flew by the Moon. Another Chinese rover mission, [Chang'e 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_4 "Chang'e 4"), achieved the first landing on [the Moon's far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon "Far side of the Moon") in early 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-332) Also in 2019, India successfully sent its second probe, [Chandrayaan-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-2 "Chandrayaan-2"), to the Moon. In 2020, China carried out its first robotic [sample return mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sample_return_mission "Sample return mission") ( [Chang'e 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_5 "Chang'e 5")), bringing back 1,731 grams of lunar material to Earth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-333)

The U.S. [developed plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vision_for_Space_Exploration "Vision for Space Exploration") for returning astronauts to the Moon beginning in 2004,[&#8202;](#ref-back-334-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-334) and with the signing of the U.S.-led [Artemis Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_Accords "Artemis Accords") in 2020, the [Artemis program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_program "Artemis program") aims to return humans to the Moon in the 2020s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-335) The Accords have been joined by a growing number of countries. The introduction of the Artemis Accords has fueled a renewed discussion about the international framework and cooperation of lunar activity, building on the [Moon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Treaty "Moon Treaty") and the ESA-led [Moon Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Village "Moon Village") concept.[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Space_Review_2020-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-The_Space_Review_2020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Australian_Institute_of_International_Affairs_2021-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-Australian_Institute_of_International_Affairs_2021)[&#8202;](#ref-back-The_Space_Treaty_Institute_–_Dedicated_to_Peace_and_Sustainability_in_Outer_Space._Our_Mission-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-The_Space_Treaty_Institute_–_Dedicated_to_Peace_and_Sustainability_in_Outer_Space._Our_Mission)

In 2022, South Korea launched [Danuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danuri "Danuri") successfully, its first lunar probe, from the US.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-339)
In 2023 and 2024, India and Japan became the fourth and fifth country to [soft land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing "Soft landing") a spacecraft on the Moon, following the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") and United States in the 1960s, and China in 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-340) Notably, Japan's spacecraft, the [Smart Lander for Investigating Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smart_Lander_for_Investigating_Moon "Smart Lander for Investigating Moon"), survived three lunar nights.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-341) In February 2024, the [IM-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IM-1 "IM-1") lander became the first commercially built lander to land on the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-342)

China launched [Chang'e 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_6 "Chang'e 6") in May 2024, which conducted the first sample return from the [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon "Far side of the Moon").[&#8202;](#ref-back-AJ_FI-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-AJ_FI) It also carried a Chinese rover to conduct [infrared spectroscopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absorption_spectroscopy "Absorption spectroscopy") of lunar surface.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-344) [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan") sent a lunar orbiter called [ICUBE-Q](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICUBE-Q "ICUBE-Q") along with Chang'e 6.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-345) [Nova-C 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IM-2 "IM-2"), [iSpace Lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ispace_(Japanese_company)#Hakuto-R_program "Ispace (Japanese company)") and [Blue Ghost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ghost "Blue Ghost") were all launched to the Moon in 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-346)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Orion_POV-_Destination_and_Home_%28art002e009567%29.jpg/500px-Orion_POV-_Destination_and_Home_%28art002e009567%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Orion_POV-_Destination_and_Home_(art002e009567).jpg) Photograph of the Moon, along with Earth during _[Earthset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthset "Earthset")_ and the capsule _[Integrity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_(spacecraft) "Orion (spacecraft)")_, from [cislunar space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cislunar_space "Cislunar space") during the crewed [Artemis II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_II "Artemis II") [lunar flyby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_flyby "Lunar flyby") in 2026, the first crewed circumlunar flight since 1972

NASA's [Artemis II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_II "Artemis II") crewed lunar flyby mission launched in April 2026. It was the first crewed mission around the Moon since the Apollo era. [Christina Koch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christina_Koch "Christina Koch") became the first woman, [Victor Glover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Glover "Victor Glover") the first person of color, Canadian [Jeremy Hansen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Hansen "Jeremy Hansen") the first non-American,[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-347) and commander [Reid Wiseman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reid_Wiseman "Reid Wiseman") the oldest person, to leave [low Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_orbit "Low Earth orbit") and to fly around the Moon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-348)

## Human interaction and presence

See also: [Human presence in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_presence_in_space "Human presence in space")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Moon_Soft_Landings.svg/500px-Moon_Soft_Landings.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Moon_Soft_Landings.svg) Map of all the sites of [soft landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing "Soft landing") on the Moon (2024)

Human presence on the Moon began with [remains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_artificial_objects_on_the_Moon "List of artificial objects on the Moon") of impactors, landings and [lunar orbiters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_extraterrestrial_orbiters#Moon "List of extraterrestrial orbiters"). Orbiters like the 2009-launched [Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Reconnaissance_Orbiter "Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter") continue to surveil the Moon for future missions, while landers such as the 2013-launched [Chang'e 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_3 "Chang'e 3") with its Lunar Ultraviolet Telescope are still operational as of 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-349) Five retroreflectors have been installed on the Moon since the 1970s and since used for accurate measurements of the physical [librations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration "Libration") through [laser ranging to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Laser_Ranging_experiment "Lunar Laser Ranging experiment").[&#8202;](#ref-back-UniverseToday-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-UniverseToday)

### Human impact

See also: [Space debris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_debris "Space debris"), [Space sustainability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_sustainability "Space sustainability"), [List of artificial objects on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_artificial_objects_on_the_Moon "List of artificial objects on the Moon"), [Space art §Art in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_art#Art_in_space "Space art"), [Moonbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonbase "Moonbase"), [Lunar resources §Mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_resources#Mining "Lunar resources"), [Tourism on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_on_the_Moon "Tourism on the Moon"), and [Space archaeology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_archaeology "Space archaeology")
